Abstract RiseHarness iteratively improves agent harness reliability by analyzing execution traces and integrating mechanisms from other frameworks without altering model weights.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Long-horizon terminal agents may solve a task in one run yet fail across repeated runs. Stochastic errors can propagate through actions and context, creating a gap between capability and reliable execution. We present RiseHarness, a framework for iteratively improving agent harnesses with model weights fixed. It focuses on flipping tasks with both successful and failed runs under the same configuration, using successful runs as references for failure analysis. RiseHarness combines agent trajectories with internal harness traces from a runtime monitor to examine how model actions interact with command execution, feedback, context management, and completion decisions. A Harness Handbook links this evidence to harness behaviors and source code, helping identify recurring problems in mechanisms shared across tasks. RiseHarness then searches other harness frameworks for relevant implementations and adapts them as design references to the current model and execution protocol. Candidate changes undergo behavioral checks and repeated task evaluation; versions with measured gains seed the next iteration. This loop integrates suitable mechanisms from different frameworks into a single harness and uses new execution evidence to guide further changes. We evaluate task success and reliability across models, ablate harness execution evidence and external research, and test transfer to unseen benchmarks with the same model. RiseHarness treats a specific model as the unit of harness adaptation, studying how changes to shared interaction mechanisms can make existing capabilities more reliable across tasks.

Система анализирует траектории выполнения и внутренние трейсы рантайм-монитора, находя повторяющиеся сбои в одних и тех же конфигурациях. Затем она заимствует подходящие механизмы из других фреймворков, проверяет изменения и отбирает версии с реальным приростом качества. Подход повышает надёжность агентов в длинных задачах на терминале и переносится на новые бенчмарки с той же моделью.", "emoji": "🛠️", "title": "Надёжность агента растёт не из весов модели, а из её обвязки"}

"Abstract RiseHarness iteratively improves agent harness reliability by analyzing execution traces and integrating mechanisms from other frameworks without altering model weights.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Long-horizon terminal agents may solve a task in one run yet fail across repeated runs. Stochastic errors can propagate through actions and context, creating a gap between capability and reliable execution. We present RiseHarness, a framework for iteratively improving agent harnesses with model weights fixed. It focuses on flipping tasks with both successful and failed runs under the same configuration, using successful runs as references for failure analysis. RiseHarness combines agent trajectories with internal harness traces from a runtime monitor to examine how model actions interact with command execution, feedback, context management, and completion decisions. A Harness Handbook links this evidence to harness behaviors and source code, helping identify recurring problems in mechanisms shared across tasks. RiseHarness then searches other harness frameworks for relevant implementations and adapts them as design references to the current model and execution protocol. Candidate changes undergo behavioral checks and repeated task evaluation; versions with measured gains seed the next iteration. This loop integrates suitable mechanisms from different frameworks into a single harness and uses new execution evidence to guide further changes. We evaluate task success and reliability across models, ablate harness execution evidence and external research, and test transfer to unseen benchmarks with the same model. RiseHarness treats a specific model as the unit of harness adaptation, studying how changes to shared interaction mechanisms can make existing capabilities more reliable across tasks."

Abstract Matrix-Game 4.0 separates game logic from visual synthesis via an executable engine and autoregressive video renderer, achieving real-time interactive gameplay with low latency.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Moving from controllable videos to genuinely playable worlds requires persistent world state, executable game rules, and low-latency interaction. We outline a roadmap for the next generation of Matrix-Game that separates game execution from visual generation through an executable game harness, and introduce Matrix-Game 4.0 as the first system built on this design. A lightweight game engine maintains world state and executes interaction rules, a multimodal coding agent translates player ideas into game code and dynamic scene captions, and a renderer based on autoregressive video model transforms engine-rendered low-poly proxies into detailed visuals, conditioned on dynamic captions and memory references. This framework provides continuous guidance for visual generation and enables natural-language creation and modification of scenes and gameplay. Our renderer is trained on aligned low-poly and high-fidelity video pairs and distilled into a causal generator that generates in four-frame chunks with one or two denoising steps. Together with targeted inference optimizations, Matrix-Game 4.0 enables 480p, 60 FPS gameplay on a single NVIDIA RTX5090 with a key-press-to-display latency of 112ms. Single-step generation achieves 83FPS steady-state throughput with 18.2GiB of peak GPU memory usage. With fixed prompts, steady-state throughput also reaches 67FPS on an RTX5080 and 58FPS on an RTX5070Ti. We demonstrate customizable shooter and driving games with engine-enforced mechanics, and release code, model weights, and low-poly game assets to support research and creation of playable game worlds.

Abstract EMBER predicts TCR-pMHC binding from independently folded monomers using SE(3)-invariant graphs, avoiding docking errors and reducing computation while improving accuracy across folding tools.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We propose EMBER, a structure-based framework for TCR--pMHC binding prediction that eliminates the need for a predicted docked complex, and with it the placement error that dominates such predictions. Traditional structure-based predictors utilize the co-folded complex structure of the receptor and the antigen and interpret binding from its contact surface. Consequently, every downstream feature inherits any deviation the structure-prediction tool introduced. Methodologically, EMBER folds the TCR and pMHC independently, encodes them as SE(3)-invariant residue graphs, and compares their learned representations without constructing a shared coordinate frame. A relative pose is therefore not an input of the model, so placement error cannot propagate into the prediction. Computationally, EMBER preprocesses O(N{+}M) monomer folds where a complex-based predictor needs O(NM) operations. Experimentally, under epitope-disjoint evaluation with six retrained baselines, EMBER is first on every metric under all four folding tools, by 0.9 to 14.4% in micro-AUROC, 18.5 to 25.3% in micro-AUPRC and 10.6 to 24.1% in MCC. Its ranking is also up to 85% more stable across folding tools.

Модель независимо предсказывает 3D-структуры TCR и pMHC и кодирует их в виде SE(3)-инвариантных графов остатков. Abstract Anti-Persistence improves LLM agent self-improvement by accumulating execution experience while discarding synthesized guidance after use, reducing error reproduction and cost.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small LLM agents can improve across tasks without updating model parameters by storing experience from their own executions in external memory and reusing it to guide later decisions. A central design question is whether to retain only observed trajectories and outcomes or also the rules, summaries, and skills inferred from them. Retaining these abstractions allows agents to reuse strategies without repeatedly deriving them from raw trajectories. However, errors in stored abstractions can propagate to subsequent decisions through retrieval and reuse. They can also shape execution trajectories, which then become the basis for new abstractions that reproduce the original errors. We term this feedback mechanism error reproduction: errors are regenerated through a cycle of abstraction, execution, and re-abstraction, extending their persistence beyond direct reuse of the original faulty knowledge. We propose Anti-Persistence, a memory design that accumulates trajectories and outcomes while reconstructing task-specific guidance on demand. An adaptive selector uses prior outcomes to decide whether and how to synthesize guidance, which is discarded after execution. This prevents its direct reuse as a stored object, although indirect feedback through retained trajectories remains possible. Across different benchmarks and backbones, Anti-Persistence achieves higher overall held-out success and the lowest average LLM-call cost among the evaluated memory designs. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract Anti-Persistence improves LLM agent self-improvement by accumulating execution experience while discarding synthesized guidance after use, reducing error reproduction and cost.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small LLM agents can improve across tasks without updating model parameters by storing experience from their own executions in external memory and reusing it to guide later decisions. A central design question is whether to retain only observed trajectories and outcomes or also the rules, summaries, and skills inferred from them. Retaining these abstractions allows agents to reuse strategies without repeatedly deriving them from raw trajectories. However, errors in stored abstractions can propagate to subsequent decisions through retrieval and reuse. They can also shape execution trajectories, which then become the basis for new abstractions that reproduce the original errors. We term this feedback mechanism error reproduction: errors are regenerated through a cycle of abstraction, execution, and re-abstraction, extending their persistence beyond direct reuse of the original faulty knowledge. We propose Anti-Persistence, a memory design that accumulates trajectories and outcomes while reconstructing task-specific guidance on demand. An adaptive selector uses prior outcomes to decide whether and how to synthesize guidance, which is discarded after execution. This prevents its direct reuse as a stored object, although indirect feedback through retained trajectories remains possible. Across different benchmarks and backbones, Anti-Persistence achieves higher overall held-out success and the lowest average LLM-call cost among the evaluated memory designs. These results support separating experience accumulation from abstraction persistence in memorybased self-improvement."

[21.10.2026 07:06] Claude request. Model: claude-haiku-5-5. Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. Abstract The study evaluates whether Lean compilation alone ensures faithful statement autoformalization, finding substantial gaps between compiling and semantically accepted outputs across multiple systems, with tool-augmented agents improving compilation but still exhibiting real translation errors.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Lean verifies that a generated declaration is well typed, but cannot check whether it states what the user intended. We measure how far compilation overstates faithfulness in statement autoformalization without canonical Lean targets. Our criterion requires Lean compilation and agreement of two LLM judges from different model families. On an independent random audit, it agrees with the human majority on 91.5% of cases; it rarely accepts an unfaithful output but rejects some faithful ones. Across eight systems on 227 university-level statements, the gap between compiling and accepted outputs ranges from 1.3 to 29.5 percentage points: our tool-augmented agent compiles 87.2% of the statements, but only 57.7% are accepted. Human review of all the agent's rejections confirms that 23.8 of its 29.5 points are real translation errors. For the gaps of the seven one-shot systems, a two-stage review by Claude Opus 5.5 with Lean and GPT-6.1 Sol, validated on these human labels, finds that 78-100% are real translation errors for six of them. When GPT-5.2, one of the two judges, grades the translations of two models released after it, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5, it accepts 83% of the statements for each, against 21% for one-shot GPT-5.2. For these two models, the same review classifies only 4 of their 54 rejections (7%) as translation errors, so most of their measured gap is judge errors rather than translation errors. This is consistent with these systems erring less while the judge does not: the fewer errors a system makes, the smaller the share of its rejections that are translation errors. Finally, a 2^3 tool ablation of the agent shows that Lean feedback drives most of its compilation gain, and that nearly half of this gain fails the semantic criterion. Code and data are available at https://github.com/grenadeComing/beyond-compilation.

[21.10.2026 07:06] Claude request. Model: claude-haiku-5-5. Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. Abstract Compressed Window Auto-Regression partitions long answer sequences into windows with restricted autoregression and compressed latent history to prevent attention takeover and box repetition in dense vision-language grounding.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small MLLM-based detectors formulate vision-language grounding as autoregressive coordinate generation, yet in dense scenes, they often collapse into a box repetition loop, repeatedly emitting identical boxes until the decoding budget is exhausted. We trace this failure to historical-answer attention takeover: Under full-history autoregression, repeated coordinate tokens in the growing answer history attract disproportionate attention, crowding out the attention sinks needed to attend to new visual evidence. % To break this loop, in this paper, we propose Compressed Window Auto-Regression (WinAR). This new mechanism partitions a long answer sequence into consecutive generation windows and restricts full token-level autoregression to the current window only while compressing each previous window into a fixed set of latent tokens that condition subsequent decoding. This design reduces the repeated exposure to raw historical coordinates that triggers the attention takeover, yet preserves cross-window dependencies essential for coherent multi-object generation. On RSDense, which contains 200-2,000 objects per image, our model WinARDet improves RexOmni F1-score (IoU=0.5) from 32.16 to 39.36 over the baseline, while reducing the redundancy rate from 35.48% to 13.00%. WinARDet also generalizes across decoding strategies and output formats, including parallel box decoding and polygon generation, providing a practical paradigm for long-horizon vision-language grounding across scene densities.
[21.10.2026 07:06] Response: {"desc": "Авторы рассматривают задачу vision-language grounding в плотных сценах, где MLLM-детекторы генерируют координаты объектов авторегрессионно и часто зацикливаются, повторяя одинаковые боксы. Причина проблемы в том, что при полной авторегрессии по всей истории ответа повторяющиеся токены координат перетягивают на себя attention и вытесняют attention sinks, необходимые для поиска новых визуальных признаков. Предложенный механизм Compressed Window Auto-Regression (WinAR) делит длинную последовательность ответа на окна, внутри текущего окна применяет полную авторегрессию, а предыдущие окна сжимает в фиксированный набор латентных токенов. На датасете RSDense метод WinARDet повышает F1-score (IoU=0.5) с 32.16 до 39.36 и снижает долю избыточных боксов с 35.48% до 13.00%, при этом подход работает и с параллельным декодированием, и с полигонами.", "emoji": "🪟", "title": "Окна вместо бесконечного повторения: сжимаем историю, чтобы видеть новые объекты"}

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract Compressed Window Auto-Regression partitions long answer sequences into windows with restricted autoregression and compressed latent history to prevent attention takeover and box repetition in dense vision-language grounding.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small MLLM-based detectors formulate vision-language grounding as autoregressive coordinate generation, yet in dense scenes, they often collapse into a box repetition loop, repeatedly emitting identical boxes until the decoding budget is exhausted. We trace this failure to historical-answer attention takeover: Under full-history autoregression, repeated coordinate tokens in the growing answer history attract disproportionate attention, crowding out the attention sinks needed to attend to new visual evidence. % To break this loop, in this paper, we propose Compressed Window Auto-Regression (WinAR). This new mechanism partitions a long answer sequence into consecutive generation windows and restricts full token-level autoregression to the current window only while compressing each previous window into a fixed set of latent tokens that condition subsequent decoding. This design reduces the repeated exposure to raw historical coordinates that triggers the attention takeover, yet preserves cross-window dependencies essential for coherent multi-object generation. On RSDense, which contains 200-2,000 objects per image, our model WinARDet improves RexOmni F1-score (IoU=0.5) from 32.16 to 39.36 over the baseline, while reducing the redundancy rate from 35.48% to 13.00%. WinARDet also generalizes across decoding strategies and output formats, including parallel box decoding and polygon generation, providing a practical paradigm for long-horizon vision-language grounding across scene densities."

Abstract EvaOmni is an evidence-grounded benchmark that evaluates whether omnimodal language models identify audio-visual evidence and construct reasoning chains for audiovisual questions.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Omni LLMs can naturally locate key audio and visual evidence and integrate information across modalities for audiovisual reasoning. Existing audio-visual reasoning benchmarks that mainly rely on multiple-choice questions usually score models solely based on answer correctness, making it difficult to assess whether a model truly forms a reasoning chain from evidence to the correct answer. In addition, textual or unimodal shortcuts may allow models to answer correctly without analyzing the necessary audio-visual evidence, further weakening the reliability of evaluation benchmarks. To address these issues, we introduce EvaOmni, an evidence-grounded benchmark containing 392 videos and 1,027 purely human-annotated question-answer pairs, covering 15 capabilities and three levels: retrieval and localization, basic understanding, and complex reasoning. We design a series of rigorous annotation procedures in which experts annotate audio and visual evidence and construct video-based distractors; humans review whether questions meet strict inspection criteria, and finally models filter textual and unimodal shortcuts. Beyond high-quality question-answer pairs, we require models to simultaneously return the answer and supporting evidence intervals in order to evaluate the complete reasoning chain. We propose the Coverage Grounded Score (C.G.) to measure how much annotated evidence a model identifies, and the Bottleneck Grounded Score (B.G.) to penalize poor temporal localization in either modality; both are incorporated into answer correctness. Across 7 open-weight and 17 proprietary Omni LLMs, Gemini3.7-Flash achieved the highest overall accuracy (79.4%), C.G. (70.8%), and B.G. (35.1%). At Level 3, it reached 79.1% accuracy, but its B.G. was only 12.3%. These results show that even the most advanced models still have room for development in grounded audio-visual reasoning.

Датасет включает 392 видео и 1027 вопросов с ручной разметкой, дистракторами и фильтрацией текстовых и унимодальных шорткатов. Для оценки вводятся метрики Coverage Grounded Score (C.G.) и Bottleneck Grounded Score (B.G.), учитывающие полноту найденных доказательств и качество временной локализации. Результаты 24 моделей показывают, что даже лучшие системы, например Gemini3.7-Flash, плохо справляются с обоснованным audio-visual reasoning, несмотря на высокую точность ответов.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Правильный ответ — не повод доверять модели: проверяем цепочку доказательств"}

Abstract A large-scale continuous flow language model achieves efficient training, high-quality generation, and faster inference through blockwise flow design, self-speculative decoding, and multi-token prediction.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Continuous flow language models offer a path to parallel generation with tools for control and acceleration unavailable to discrete diffusion, yet effective techniques for building them at the billion-parameter scale remain less established. We present Lafa (Large flow language model), a fast, high-performing flow language model scaled to 8B parameters in a data-efficient manner. Lafa is a blockwise flow model with causal attention on the prefix and bidirectional, asynchronous flow within each block. This design enables efficient training by adapting pretrained autoregressive (AR) models and fast inference via prefix caching and blockwise parallel decoding. We train Lafa at 0.5B, 7B, and 8B scales with fewer than 30B tokens, more efficient than previous diffusion models, and evaluate it on instruction following, reasoning, and coding, where the learned flow matches or surpasses the quality of AR and can be distilled into flow maps for one- and few-step generation. Notably, flow training preserves or even improves pretrained AR capabilities via a multi-token prediction effect, which we leverage in a self-speculative decoding scheme that verifies flow-based drafts autoregressively within a single model. This yields 2.54x the throughput of an AR model at comparable quality, outperforming multi-token prediction and discrete diffusion methods. Our findings show that continuous flows can serve as a scalable foundation for language modeling.
[21.10.2026 07:06] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлена Lafa — языковая модель на основе непрерывных потоков (flow language model) с 8 миллиардами параметров, которая обучается эффективно за счёт блочного дизайна и адаптации предобученных AR-моделей. Модель использует причинное внимание на префиксе и двунаправленный асинхронный flow внутри каждого блока, что позволяет быстро генерировать текст с помощью кэширования префикса и блочного параллельного декодирования. Авторы показывают, что качество Lafa на задачах следования инструкциям, рассуждений и программирования не уступает AR-моделям, а дистилляция в flow maps даёт генерацию за один-несколько шагов. "Abstract A large-scale continuous flow language model achieves efficient training, high-quality generation, and faster inference through blockwise flow design, self-speculative decoding, and multi-token prediction.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Continuous flow language models offer a path to parallel generation with tools for control and acceleration unavailable to discrete diffusion, yet effective techniques for building them at the billion-parameter scale remain less established. We present Lafa (Large flow language model), a fast, high-performing flow language model scaled to 8B parameters in a data-efficient manner. Lafa is a blockwise flow model with causal attention on the prefix and bidirectional, asynchronous flow within each block. This design enables efficient training by adapting pretrained autoregressive (AR) models and fast inference via prefix caching and blockwise parallel decoding. We train Lafa at 0.5B, 7B, and 8B scales with fewer than 30B tokens, more efficient than previous diffusion models, and evaluate it on instruction following, reasoning, and coding, where the learned flow matches or surpasses the quality of AR and can be distilled into flow maps for one- and few-step generation. Notably, flow training preserves or even improves pretrained AR capabilities via a multi-token prediction effect, which we leverage in a self-speculative decoding scheme that verifies flow-based drafts autoregressively within a single model. This yields 2.54x the throughput of an AR model at comparable quality, outperforming multi-token prediction and discrete diffusion methods. Our findings show that continuous flows can serve as a scalable foundation for language modeling."

Abstract HumanSkill-4D enables language-driven selection of avatar regions and motion intervals via shared multimodal reasoning and geometric operators without training.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We introduce avatar referring segmentation, which selects regions of animatable Gaussian avatars from language queries. A query can also specify a pose or motion to select matching intervals. This lets users interact with avatars for local editing and motion inspection. However, making these selections with existing language Gaussian splatting methods poses two challenges: adjacent parts and garments are hard to separate and may be referred to indirectly (region ambiguity), while fine-grained motions can look alike in RGB (motion ambiguity). We address these challenges with HumanSkill-4D, a training-free framework that shares a multimodal large language model (MLLM) and geometric operators across avatars. For each avatar, a semantic memory links body-part and garment categories to persistent Gaussian regions and appearance descriptions. The MLLM uses category names and descriptions to select categories by appearance, relation, or function. To find matching intervals, it composes operators into programs that check query conditions on driving joint trajectories stored in a motion memory. These category choices and operator outputs make selections traceable, while composing or adding operators supports new motion conditions. To evaluate this task, we introduce three human-annotated benchmarks and show that HumanSkill-4D outperforms the baselines in part segmentation and 3D and 4D referring segmentation, with compact memories and only minutes of setup per avatar.
[21.10.2026 07:06] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается задача референсной сегментации аватаров: по текстовому запросу выбираются нужные области анимируемого аватара на основе Gaussian splatting и интервалы движения, соответствующие описанию позы. Авторы выделяют две проблемы: неоднозначность областей (соседние части тела и одежда) и неоднозначность движений, которые похожи по RGB. Метод HumanSkill-4D работает без обучения: он использует общую мультимодальную большую языковую модель (MLLM), геометрические операторы, семантическую память для категорий частей тела и одежды и память движений по траекториям суставов. Эксперименты на трёх размеченных бенчмарках показывают превосходство над базовыми методами в сегментации частей и 3D/4D-сегментации при компактной памяти и минимальной настройке для каждого аватара.", "emoji": "🕺", "title": "Выбирай части и движения аватара просто словами, без обучения"}

Abstract TRACE organizes daily clinical events into temporal trajectories to improve inpatient risk prediction across mortality, length of stay, and readmission tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Accurate inpatient risk prediction requires modeling heterogeneous clinical evidence that accumulates irregularly over time. This challenge spans in-hospital mortality, prolonged length of stay, and readmission, each with task-specific observation windows. Existing multimodal approaches combine structured health record data, chest x-rays, and clinical text, but primarily focus on modality fusion or missing-modality compensation, with limited attention to temporal trajectories over an admission. We propose TRACE (TRAjectory modeling of multimodal Clinical Events), a daily-scale framework organizing clinical events into day-level trajectories, separating day-invariant global context from day-specific local events drawn from EHR data, chest x-rays, radiology reports, and discharge summaries. The trajectory is encoded using temporal attention, allowing unavailable modalities to emit no tokens. A phenotype-guided contrastive objective further organizes admissions under heterogeneous disease distributions. Across cohorts from MIMIC-IV, eICU, and MIMIC-III comprising nearly 180,000 total stays, we evaluate TRACE on mortality, length of stay, and readmission prediction. TRACE achieves the highest AUROC across all nine dataset-task settings and the highest ACC in eight, outperforming baselines. Ablation studies validate our attention masks and contrastive learning, while trajectory-level attribution localizes risk-associated hospital days and modalities.

Модель разделяет глобальный контекст, не зависящий от дня, и локальные события, извлекаемые из EHR, рентгенограмм грудной клетки, радиологических отчётов и выписных эпикризов. Abstract Agent S4 autonomously evolves computer-use interfaces from task experience, significantly improving open-source agent performance and reducing costs compared to larger proprietary models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Computer-use agents are limited by the interfaces humans give them. An agent may type a spreadsheet cell by cell when a single line of code would do, or print all two thousand lines of a file through a shell to find one function. Both the interfaces and the knowledge of when to use each are built by hand, which cannot keep pace with new applications, workflows, and models. We argue that interface design should itself be an agent capability. We introduce Agent S4, an autonomous computer-use scientist that builds Evolving Agent Interfaces from task experience. A meta-agent examines task agents' trajectories on synthetic training tasks, without success labels, and constructs and revises interfaces and usage knowledge in a repository. Population-based evolution then selects which repositories to develop further by their performance on validation tasks the meta-agent never sees, favoring changes that generalize beyond the seen tasks. On OSWorld V2, Agent S4, with Qwen3.8-27B and the evolved repository of interfaces, sets a new state of the art in open-source computer-use agents, reaching 21.3% binary score, 3.8 times the same model with fixed interfaces. This is above both GPT-5.5 (13.0%) and Claude Opus 4.8 (20.6%), at about 4.3% and 1.5% of their respective costs. On ScienceBoard, the evolved interfaces improve the success rate from 41.4% to 58.6% with the same model. These results suggest that useful interfaces can be evolved from experience, making autonomous interface design a source of improvement complementary to advances in models and harnesses. We open-source our code at https://github.com/simular-ai/agent-s.

Мета-агент анализирует траектории работы агентов на синтетических задачах без меток успеха и формирует репозиторий интерфейсов и знаний об их использовании. Abstract A general vision-language model can serve as a multimodal reranker via single-pass choice questions, matching or exceeding dedicated rerankers with minimal tuning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multimodal rerankers are usually dedicated models: a vision-language model (VLM) is trained on retrieval data to score the candidates of a query. Scoring options is not specific to reranking. System One decision models such as Jev map a state and a typed question to one probability per option without generating text, and general models can make such decisions with little or no training. Reranking is one such decision, with the query and its candidates as the state and the candidates as the options. We therefore argue that a general VLM can serve as a multimodal reranker through System One decisions, and that decision tuning can improve its reranking. We ask a VLM one choice question over the top 16 candidates of a first-stage retriever and rank the candidates by the probabilities of their labels, read from one forward pass. On MMEB-V2 image retrieval, this choice scores 5.4 to 7.3 hit@1 above a yes/no question per candidate, and the yes/no question falls below the first stage. With the choice, zero-shot Qwen3.8-27B beats the supervised Qwen3-VL-Reranker-2B on all three benchmarks and comes within 1.6 points of Reranker-8B on each. It matches Reranker-8B on MMEB-V2 visual documents (84.79 vs. 84.78 nDCG@5), at 1.1 to 1.5 times its latency. JEV-27B-VL, the same backbone tuned on text decisions only, improves page reranking by 0.6 to 1.1 points, with gains on most tasks (sign test, p <= 0.017), and leaves natural images unchanged. The zero-shot model is larger than the supervised rerankers and of a newer generation, so these comparisons are confounded; we discuss this and other alternative views, and the experiments that would test the position.
[21.10.2026 07:07] Response: {"desc": "Авторы показывают, что универсальная VLM (vision-language model) может работать как мультимодальный реранкер без специального обучения: модели задаётся один вопрос с выбором из 16 кандидатов, а ранжирование строится по вероятностям вариантов за один проход (System One decision). Такой подход на MMEB-V2 превосходит вопрос «да/нет» для каждого кандидата и почти догоняет специализированные рерankerы, в том числе обученные на retrieval-данных. Дополнительная настройка на текстовых решениях (модель JEV-27B-VL) улучшает ранжирование визуальных документов, не меняя результаты на естественных изображениях. Авторы отмечают, что сравнение осложнено разницей в размере и поколении моделей, и предлагают эксперименты для проверки своей гипотезы.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Универсальная VLM справляется с реранкингом одним вопросом с выбором"}

Abstract A typed decision model consulted at fixed points increased agent costs without improving success rates, despite better ranking of finish requests, due to poor calibration.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agent scaffolds increasingly delegate small decisions (whether to stop, verify, or change approach) to cheaper models. We ask whether a typed decision model, Jev 1.13, consulted only at deterministic decision points, makes a Claude Sonnet 5.5 terminal agent cheaper without making it worse. In a pre-specified study on Terminal-Bench 2.1, we hold one agent loop fixed and vary only the controller: none, deterministic rules, a small LLM (Claude Haiku 5.5) answering the same questions, Jev, and a placebo that samples Jev's development-phase choice frequencies at the same moments. On 21 held-out tasks (315 trials), Jev raised cost per attempt by 19% (geometric-mean ratio 1.19, 95% CI 1.02-1.36) at an identical pass rate (73.0% vs. 73.0%). Jev was cheaper than the small-LLM controller (ratio 0.81) but 22% more expensive than the placebo, and no pass-rate difference between arms was detectable. Offline, Jev ranked finish requests better than the alternatives (AUROC 0.78 vs. 0.62 for rules and 0.60 for the small LLM) but was poorly calibrated: its mean probability that a run was complete was 0.50 when 87% of those runs passed, so it asked the agent to verify work that was already correct. No controller ever chose to abandon a run. Informative decision signals did not become useful interventions; we discuss what that would require.
